Taking a Look at the Combined Reports Data

Total number of coded specifications (rows) and unique analyses (analysis_id).

total_obs <- nrow(df)

analyses <- df %>% 
  group_by(author) %>% 
  summarize(analyses_per_paper = max(analysis_id)) %>%
  summarize(total_n_analyses = sum(analyses_per_paper))

The adjudicated sample consisted of 3552 analysis specifications, divided across 506 unique analyses and 17 different papers.

Judgement per rater

# aux vector
col <- c("human_match_judgement",
         "codebot_match_judgement", 
         "codebot_human_agreement")

# ratings for each category separately
matches <- tibble(
  variable = col,
  match = map_dbl(df[col], 
                  ~sum(.x == 1, na.rm = TRUE)),
  mismatch = map_dbl(df[col], 
                     ~sum(.x == 0, na.rm = TRUE)),
  undecided = map_dbl(df[col], 
                      ~sum(.x == 99, na.rm = TRUE)),
  "NA" = map_dbl(df[col], 
                 ~sum(is.na(.x)))
)

Q1: Examining Frequency of Matchingness

How often does the narrative description of statistical analyses in the manuscript match the analyses reported in the analytic scripts according to the human and CodeBot rating, respectively? → Descriptive examination of (mis-)matches for human and CodeBot ratings.

Inspection of Raw CodeBot Output Sample

# check for any empty rows
check_empty <- df_base %>% 
  group_by(paper_value) %>% 
  count() %>% 
  filter(paper_value == "" | is.na(paper_value)) %>% 
  pull()

# count judgments + percentages
baser <- tibble(
  value = c("match","mismatch","unknown"),
  codebot_count = map_dbl(value, 
                        ~df_base %>% 
                          filter(match_status == .x) %>% 
                          nrow()
  ),
  codebot_percent = map_dbl(codebot_count,
                                ~round_up((.x/nrow(df_base) * 100), 
                                          digits = 2)))
baser %>% 
  kable() %>% 
  kable_classic(full_width = FALSE)
value codebot_count codebot_percent
match 1785 59.74
mismatch 157 5.25
unknown 1046 35.01 
df_base %>% 
  count(dimension)

  dimension                        n
  <chr>                        <int>

In total, CodeBot coded 2988 observations. This corresponds to 747 unique analyses.

Inspection of Adjudicated Sample

# how many observations in total were coded not NA by the raters
h_total_rated <- df %>% 
  filter(!is.na(human_match_judgement)) %>% 
  nrow()

c_total_rated <- df %>% 
  filter(!is.na(codebot_match_judgement)) %>% 
  nrow()

mr <- tibble(
  value = c("1","0","99"),
  human_count = map_dbl(value, 
                        ~df %>% 
                          filter(human_match_judgement == .x) %>% 
                          nrow()
  ),
  human_count_percent = map_dbl(human_count,
                                ~round_up((.x/h_total_rated * 100), 
                                          digits = 2)),
  
  codebot_count = map_dbl(value, 
                          ~df %>% 
                            filter(codebot_match_judgement == .x) %>% 
                            nrow()
  ),
  codebot_count_percent = map_dbl(codebot_count,
                                  ~round_up((.x/c_total_rated * 100), 
                                            digits = 2)))

mr %>% 
  kable(caption = "RQ1 Judgement Rates Per Rater (Adjudicated Sample)") %>%
  kable_classic(full_width = FALSE)
RQ1 Judgement Rates Per Rater (Adjudicated Sample)
value human_count human_count_percent codebot_count codebot_count_percent
1 1987 76.90 1258 71.15
0 512 19.81 68 3.85
99 85 3.29 442 25.00

The adjudicated sample contains 2584 (72.75%) observations coded by the human and 1768 (49.77%) observations by coded by CodeBot.

delta <- nrow(df_base)- c_total_rated

delta_p <- round_up(delta/nrow(df_base)*100, digits = 2)

Furthermore, the adjudicator only matched 59.17% of the total raw CodeBot output to human judgments.

[1] 100
c_total_rated == sum(mr$codebot_count)

Q2: GLMM Agreement Estimation

How high is the estimated agreement of the human and CodeBot ratings of the judgement of (mis-)matches between the narrative description of statistical main analyses and the respective analytic code? → GLMM estimating agreement while considering nesting effects per paper?

# sampe size
both <- df %>% 
  filter(exclude == FALSE & 
           !is.na(codebot_human_agreement)) %>% 
  nrow()

both_na <- df %>% 
  filter(exclude == TRUE &
           is.na(codebot_human_agreement)) %>% 
  nrow()

model <- df %>% 
  # exclude data, see processing
  filter(exclude == FALSE & !is.na(codebot_human_agreement))%>% 
  # rename variable to match preregistered formula
  rename(agree = codebot_human_agreement) %>% 
  glmer(agree ~ 1 + (1|paper_id),
        data = .,
        family = binomial)

results <- report(model)
# convert to percentage
intercept_percent <- round_up(plogis(fixef(model)[1]) * 100, 
                              digits = 2)

ci_percent <- map_dbl(c(1,2),
                      ~round_up(plogis(confint(model, 
                                               parm = "beta_", 
                                               method = "Wald")[.x]) * 100, 
                                digits = 2
                      ))

Out of the 3552 total possible specifications, 1011 observations were coded by both rater. This reflects 28.46% of the entire adjudicated sample and 39.13% of all observations coded by me.

EasyStats Results Report: “We fitted a constant (intercept-only) logistic mixed model (estimated using ML and Nelder-Mead optimizer) to predict agree (formula: agree ~ 1). The model included paper_id as random effect (formula: ~1 | paper_id). The model’s intercept is at 3.08 (95% CI [2.34, 3.83], p < .001).

Standardized parameters were obtained by fitting the model on a standardized version of the dataset. 95% Confidence Intervals (CIs) and p-values were computed using a Wald z-distribution approximation.”

In probability terms: The probability of CodeBot’s judgement agreeing with the human judgment is estimated to be 95.62% (95%CI [91.2, 97.87]).

Q3: Exploratory Examination of Reasons for Missingness

What could explain why CodeBot coded analyses the human did not code and vice versa? → Qualitative examination of 3% randomly sampled specifications that were code by CodeBot or the human coding only.

Overview 3% Sample Entries Only Coded by the Human

# subset relevant entries
q3_h <- df %>% 
  filter(is.na(codebot_manuscript_content) & !is.na(human_manuscript_content))

# determine number of relevant instances
n_only_human <- nrow(q3_h)

# determine size of sample by calculating 3% from the previously drawn sample
size_h <- round_up(n_only_human * 0.03, 
                   digits = 0) 

# indexes for the 3 % sampled rows
index_only_human <- sample(c(1:n_only_human), 
                           size_h,
                           replace = FALSE)

sample_only_human <- q3_h %>% 
  slice(index_only_human)

sample_only_human %>%
  select(author, paper_id, analysis_id, dimension, human_manuscript_content, human_manuscript_location, human_code_content, human_code_location) %>% 
  kable(format = "html") %>% 
  kable_classic(full_width = FALSE) %>% 
  scroll_box(width = "100%", height = "400px")

Pre-registered analysis outcome: In this sample of 37/1249, i.e. 3% of all the cases, the majority of entries missing by CodeBot are of the category “variable specification” (22/37). The number of missing entries by CodeBot is higher in cases where the human rater noted crucial manuscript information to be located in a table (2) rather than the text (0).

nguyen 14 93 transformation Study 4 regressed a general positive-impression measure (two valence items) on target behavior, CWV, and their interaction. The behavior × CWV interaction was significant (b = 0.44, t(377) = 4.13, p < .001), indicating that competitive worldviews moderated halo-like liking of antagonistic vs. affiliative managers. General Positive Impression. Participants rated two items on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree, 5 = strongly agree): the extent to which they had a positive impression and a negative impression of the target (α = .96). They also rated the target on the same 5-point scale on the positive dimensions of the Big 5 personality traits (e.g., “warm, sympathetic,” “dependable, self-disciplined”), from the Ten Item Personality Inventory (TIPI; Gosling et al., 2003; α = .90). These two sets of ratings were examined separately as independent measures of general positive impression. nguyen 14 101 transformation A regression in Study 5 predicted general competence from CWV, proximal impact, and a TIPI-based positive-impression index. Both proximal impact (b = 0.19, p < .01) and TIPI-based impression (b = 0.93, p < .001) were significant, indicating dual mediators (Table 8, Model 7). General Competence. The same general competence scale was used as in Studies 3 and 4 ( α = .91). General Positive Impression. Participants rated the extent to which they had a positive impression and negative impression of the manager (α = .93), in addition to rating the manager on the positive dimensions of the TIPI (α = .82), all on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree,5 = strongly agree). As in Study 4, these two sets of ratings were examined separately as independent measures of general positive impression. Proximal Impact. Participants rated two items capturing the extent to which they saw the manager’s communication as likely to produce the desired outcomes (raise the “motivation and work ethic of the restaurant staffers,” cause the restaurant staffers to “be more likely to work their scheduled shifts”) on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree,5 = strongly agree; α = .80). CWV. The same CWV scale was used as in all previous studies (α = .80; M = 2.72, SD = 0.79). evans 8 30 transformation A Pearson correlation tested the relationship between locus of control and unethical behavioural intentions in the direct replication. The small-to-moderate positive correlation (r = .14, p < .01) indicates that a more external work locus of control was associated with greater intention to engage in unethical expense inflation. The categorical independent variables (quality of work experience, peer influence, managerial influence and gender) were dummy coded before being regressed upon behavioural intention alongside Machiavellianism and locus of control total scores. Locus of Control, Machiavellianism, and Social Desirability – Concurrent with the original study, ten items of the Rotter (1966), Christie and Geis (1970) and Crowne and Marlowe (1960) scales (respectively) were adopted using a 5-point likert scale response. Unethical Behavioural Intention was captured by four items assessed through a 5-point likert scale. nguyen 14 65 transformation Using PROCESS Model 8 in Study 4, the authors tested a moderated mediation model with general competence as the outcome, target behavior as the predictor, CWV as a moderator of the a-paths, and proximal impact plus general positive impression as parallel mediators. Conditional indirect effects through both mediators were strongest at low CWV and weakest at high CWV, and the index of moderated mediation for each mediator was significantly different from zero (e.g., 0.16 for proximal impact, 95% CI [0.07, 0.27]). General Positive Impression. Participants rated two items on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree, 5 = strongly agree): the extent to which they had a positive impression and a negative impression of the target (α = .96). They also rated the target on the same 5-point scale on the positive dimensions of the Big 5 personality traits (e.g., “warm, sympathetic,” “dependable, self-disciplined”), from the Ten Item Personality Inventory (TIPI; Gosling et al., 2003; α = .90). These two sets of ratings were examined separately as independent measures of general positive impression. Proximal Behavioral Impact. Participants rated four items measuring the extent to which they saw the target’s behavior as likely to produce the desired outcomes (e.g., raise the sales team’s motivation and performance) on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree, 5 = strongly agree; α = .95). Participants completed a 10-item measure of CWV (e.g., “It is a dog-eat-dog world where you have to be ruthless at times,” “My knowledge and experience tell me that the social world we live in is basically a competitive ‘jungle’ in which the fittest survive and succeed, in which power, wealth, and winning are everything, and might is right,” “Life is not governed by the ‘survival of the fittest.’ We should let compassion and moral laws be our guide,” reverse-scored; Perry et al., 2013). A linear regression was conducted predicting competence evaluations of the target on a dummy variable indicating target behavior (0 = affiliative, 1 = antagonistic), participant CWV, and their interaction. bloy 5 5 transformation In Study 1, a paired-samples t-test evaluated differences in the person-fit lpzm index between gibberish and content scales. The lpzm index, reflecting the likelihood of a response pattern under an IRT model, was compared to see if gibberish responses appeared more or less consistent with the model than content responses (M_gibberish = 0.81, M_content = -0.69). The difference was statistically significant, t(619) = 15.0, p < .001, Cohen’s d = 0.83. The data set originally contained 32 columns comprising two types of scales: 16 columns with gibberish questions and 16 columns with content questions. We transformed the data into long format by stacking the 32 columns into 16 columns representing the questions and adding a 17th column to indicate whether each row originated from the gibberish or content scale. The analysis included six steps. First, we calculated six indexes of careless responses for each row: long string, standard deviation, the correlation between two half scales, Mahalanobis distance by the R package careless (Yentes & Wilhelm, 2021), and two person-fit indices by the R package PerFit (Tendeiro et al., 2016). Second, we ran paired-samples t tests comparing these careless-responses-index scores among the content items with the gibberish items. Before conducting these t tests, we removed two respondents who did not have both content and gibberish responses to ensure the analysis included only complete data from all participants. NA irtdata <- data_study_1[, 1:16]-1 data_study_1$gpoly <- Gpoly(matrix = irtdata, Ncat = 7)$PFscores data_study_1$lzpoly <- lzpoly(matrix = irtdata, Ncat = 7)$PFscores ... # Deleting two respondents who do not have both types of scales, # so that we can do a paired samples t-test counts <- table(data_study_1$Participant_ID) single_row_ids <- names(counts[counts < 2]) filtered_data <- data_study_1[!data_study_1$Participant_ID %in% single_row_ids, ] filtered_data <- filtered_data %>% arrange(Participant_ID, condition) ... filtered_data$gpoly <- unlist(filtered_data$gpoly); filtered_data$lzpoly <- unlist(filtered_data$lzpoly) t.test(data = filtered_data, lzpoly ~ condition, paired = TRUE) cohen.d(data = filtered_data, lzpoly ~ condition, paired = TRUE) NA

avilles 2 2 transformation Spearman rank-order correlation between the cohort-level prevalence of singles at T1 and mean singlehood satisfaction at T1 across cohorts and age groups. The analysis tested whether cohorts with a higher share of singles also showed higher singlehood satisfaction, finding a positive association, rs(7,488) = .20, p < .001. Second, we calculated the proportion of singles at T1 (Wave 1 for earlier-born cohort and Wave 11 for later-born cohort), which was done with the prevalence sample of N = 17,697 participants. Higher values indicated a greater share of singles at T1 in the respective cohort. We tested differences between the later-born and earlier-born cohort in the prevalence of singles (Hypothesis 1) using a chi-square test and computed Spearman’s rank correlations to assess the association between the prevalence of singles and satisfaction at T1 (Hypothesis 2). ../../data/raw/main/adjudicator/combined_reports/evans_combined_report.csv evans 8 30 transformation NA 0;0 NA NA NA A Pearson correlation tested the relationship between locus of control and unethical behavioural intentions in the direct replication. The small-to-moderate positive correlation (r = .14, p < .01) indicates that a more external work locus of control was associated with greater intention to engage in unethical expense inflation. The categorical independent variables (quality of work experience, peer influence, managerial influence and gender) were dummy coded before being regressed upon behavioural intention alongside Machiavellianism and locus of control total scores. Locus of Control, Machiavellianism, and Social Desirability – Concurrent with the original study, ten items of the Rotter (1966), Christie and Geis (1970) and Crowne and Marlowe (1960) scales (respectively) were adopted using a 5-point likert scale response. Unethical Behavioural Intention was captured by four items assessed through a 5-point likert scale. CWV remained a significant positive predictor of perceived effectiveness of antagonistic behavior above and beyond these covariates. Behavioral Impact. Participants were presented with 16 behaviors and indicated the "typical impact" of each behavior "on a person’s ability to get things done when they ’re interacting and working with other people," using a 5-point scale (1 = would greatly decrease their ability to get things done ,5 = would greatly increase their ability to get things done ). ... Our main analyses concerned 10 antagonistic behaviors (e.g., "being abrasive and blunt toward others," "calling out and blaming people," "making threats or ultimatums"; α = .92). ... CWV. Participants completed a 10-item measure of CWV (e.g., "It is a dog-eat-dog world where you have to be ruthless at times," "My knowledge and experience tell me that the social world we live in is basically a competitive 'jungle' in which the fittest survive and succeed, in which power, wealth, and winning are everything, and might is right," "Life is not governed by the 'survival of the fittest.' We should let compassion and moral laws be our guide," reverse-scored; Perry et al., 2013).4 Participants responded on a 7-point scale (1 = strongly disagree ,7 = strongly agree ; α = .84; M = 2.74, SD = 0.96). The same general competence scale was used as in Studies 3 and 4 ( α = .91). General Positive Impression. Participants rated the extent to which they had a positive impression and negative impression of the manager (α = .93), in addition to rating the manager on the positive dimensions of the TIPI (α = .82), all on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree,5 = strongly agree). As in Study 4, these two sets of ratings were examined separately as independent measures of general positive impression. Proximal Impact. Participants rated two items capturing the extent to which they saw the manager’s communication as likely to produce the desired outcomes (raise the “motivation and work ethic of the restaurant staffers,” cause the restaurant staffers to “be more likely to work their scheduled shifts”) on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree,5 = strongly agree; α = .80). ../../data/raw/main/adjudicator/combined_reports/nguyen_combined_report.csv nguyen 14 65 variable specification SDO 2 # read in anonymized data #### toe_data <- read.csv('study1_anon.csv') [...] # correlations #### toe_data %>% select(CWV, DWV, cynicism, coop_primal, confl_temp_best, confl_temp_worst, ZSB, gen_trust, SDO, RWA, ILT, coerc_TOP, collab_TOP) %>% sjPlot::tab_corr(na.deletion='pairwise', triangle='lower', digits=2, string.diag=rep('-',13), file='study1_kitchensink_corr.doc') Savvy study1.R:40-41;139-144 1 Using PROCESS Model 8 in Study 4, the authors tested a moderated mediation model with general competence as the outcome, target behavior as the predictor, CWV as a moderator of the a-paths, and proximal impact plus general positive impression as parallel mediators. Conditional indirect effects through both mediators were strongest at low CWV and weakest at high CWV, and the index of moderated mediation for each mediator was significantly different from zero (e.g., 0.16 for proximal impact, 95% CI [0.07, 0.27]). Proximal behavioral impact and positive impression were then simultaneously input into a bootstrapped mediation analysis with 5,000 iterations to test whether they each mediated the path, which was moderated by participants’ CWV, from target behavior to general competence ratings (PROCESS Model 8; Hayes, 2013 ). Consistent with our prediction, the conditional indirect effect of target antagonism on evaluations through perceived proximal behavioral impact was strongest for participants with lower CWV and weakest for participants higher in CWV (see Table 7). The same pattern was observed for both measures of positive impression: The conditional indirect effect was strongest for participants with lower CWV and weakest in participants with higher CWV. They also rated the target on the same 5-point scale on the positive dimensions of the Big 5 personality traits (e.g., “warm, sympathetic,” “dependable, self-disciplined”), from the Ten Item Personality Inventory (TIPI; Gosling et al., 2003; α = .90). These two sets of ratings were examined separately as independent measures of general positive impression. Proximal Behavioral Impact. Participants rated four items measuring the extent to which they saw the target’s behavior as likely to produce the desired outcomes (e.g., raise the sales team’s motivation and performance) on a 5-point scale (1 = strongly disagree, 5 = strongly agree; α = .95). Participants completed a 10-item measure of CWV (e.g., “It is a dog-eat-dog world where you have to be ruthless at times,” “My knowledge and experience tell me that the social world we live in is basically a competitive ‘jungle’ in which the fittest survive and succeed, in which power, wealth, and winning are everything, and might is right,” “Life is not governed by the ‘survival of the fittest.’ We should let compassion and moral laws be our guide,” reverse-scored; Perry et al., 2013). CodeBot quotes: “The actual recall was calculated by multiplying the percent correct recognition by 100 to provide a comparison between JOLs (predicted) and percent correct (actual) recognition.” However, the model compares the accuracy rate which is not the same as the actual recognition.

It examines whether individual response patterns align with overall sample trends or IRT-model predictions. The method includes several indices, two of which we use. … The second index is lpzm (standardized likelihood). It is a calculation of the likelihood of a response pattern based on the participant’s trait level in the IRT model.” “First, we calculated six indexes of careless responses for each row: long string, standard deviation, the correlation between two half scales, Mahalanobis distance by the R package careless (Yentes & Wilhelm, 2021), and two person-fit indices by the R package PerFit (Tendeiro et al., 2016). ../../data/raw/main/adjudicator/combined_reports/bloy_combined_report.csv bloy 5 5 transformation NA NA NA NA NA In Study 1, a paired-samples t-test evaluated differences in the person-fit lpzm index between gibberish and content scales. The lpzm index, reflecting the likelihood of a response pattern under an IRT model, was compared to see if gibberish responses appeared more or less consistent with the model than content responses (M_gibberish = 0.81, M_content = -0.69). The difference was statistically significant, t(619) = 15.0, p < .001, Cohen’s d = 0.83. The data set originally contained 32 columns comprising two types of scales: 16 columns with gibberish questions and 16 columns with content questions. We transformed the data into long format by stacking the 32 columns into 16 columns representing the questions and adding a 17th column to indicate whether each row originated from the gibberish or content scale. The analysis included six steps. First, we calculated six indexes of careless responses for each row: long string, standard deviation, the correlation between two half scales, Mahalanobis distance by the R package careless (Yentes & Wilhelm, 2021), and two person-fit indices by the R package PerFit (Tendeiro et al., 2016). Second, we ran paired-samples t tests comparing these careless-responses-index scores among the content items with the gibberish items. Before conducting these t tests, we removed two respondents who did not have both content and gibberish responses to ensure the analysis included only complete data from all participants. The difference was statistically significant, t(619) = 15.0, p < .001, Cohen’s d = 0.83. The data set originally contained 32 columns comprising two types of scales: 16 columns with gibberish questions and 16 columns with content questions. We transformed the data into long format by stacking the 32 columns into 16 columns representing the questions and adding a 17th column to indicate whether each row originated from the gibberish or content scale. The analysis included six steps. First, we calculated six indexes of careless responses for each row: long string, standard deviation, the correlation between two half scales, Mahalanobis distance by the R package careless (Yentes & Wilhelm, 2021), and two person-fit indices by the R package PerFit (Tendeiro et al., 2016). Second, we ran paired-samples t tests comparing these careless-responses-index scores among the content items with the gibberish items. Before conducting these t tests, we removed two respondents who did not have both content and gibberish responses to ensure the analysis included only complete data from all participants. Since the adjudicator did not keep the explanations provided by CodeBot in the results file, it is necessary to go back to “./data/raw/main/codebot/output” and identify each individual case by hand (see: setup chunk at the top).

Carvalho et el.

test <- disagree_only %>% 
  filter(author == "carvalho" & analysis_id == 1) %>%  
  select(codebot_manuscript_content,analysis_id) %>%  
  pull(codebot_manuscript_content)

carvalho %>% 
  filter(analysis_id == 1 & paper_value == test) %>% 
  select(dimension, brief_description, paper_value, code_value, explanation) %>% 
  kable(format = "html") %>% 
  kable_classic(full_width = FALSE) %>% 
  scroll_box(width = "100%", height = "400px")
dimension brief_description paper_value code_value explanation
Inference Specification A two-sample t-test compared overall pretest accuracy (proportion of 12 items correct, including critical and catch items) between children in the Generating Predictions condition and those in the Retrieve condition to verify baseline equivalence of prior knowledge. The test evaluated whether the mean proportion correct differed between conditions before the lesson. To investigate these differences, we used a two-sample t-test for null hypothesis significance testing, and an equivalence test, via two one-sided tests with α-level of .05. These tested the null hypotheses that true mean difference is equal to 0 (null hypothesis significance testing), and true mean difference is more extreme than −0.1667 and 0.1667, equivalent to one question difference in the pretest, on average, between the two conditions (two one-sided tests). t.test(data$Pre.test.overall[data$Condition=="GENERATE"],data$Pre.test.overall[data$Condition=="RETRIEVE"],var.equal = T) The paper specifies using both a null hypothesis t-test and an equivalence test via two one-sided tests with α = .05, but the code only runs a standard two-sample t-test without implementing the equivalence (TOST) procedure or explicitly setting the α-level. Therefore, the inference procedures described in the paper are not fully reflected in the provided code.

While there was no problem with the correct identification for the model specification, CodeBot rated the absence of the TOST snippet as proof of mismatch between the specified alpha level for the t-test and the code snippet in the inference criteria. However, this is irrelevant to the t-test actually conducted, since the t-test and the equivalence test are not the same. I rated this as a match since an alpha level of .05 is standard in the t-test function. Since the default function was used, it was counted as a match. CodeBot problem -> Differentiating between analyses which are intertwined but not identical. –> Which info needs to be stated explicitly and which can be counted as automatically implied due to e.g. reporting standards in the field?

Here, CodeBot might have spotted an error which I missed by pointing out that different variables/data sets for different groups are employed. –> Because no author really posted ALL material to the repo (e.g. also due to privacy concerns) it was never 100% clear which variable came from where and why exactly there was a new data set.

nguyen 103 A parallel regression in Study 4 predicted leadership effectiveness from target behavior, CWV, their interaction, and proximal impact. Proximal impact again strongly predicted leadership effectiveness (b = 1.04, p < .001), and attenuated the interaction effect (Table 6, Model 4). Variable Specification Proximal behavioral impact positively predicted general competence and leadership effectiveness ratings of the target, even when target behavior, participants’ CWV, and their interaction were accounted for (see Table 6, Models 3 and 4). # all constructs #### m1 <- lm(gen_competent ~ belligerent * CWV_ctr, study4_data); summary(m1); confint(m1) m2 <- lm(org_competent ~ belligerent * CWV_ctr, study4_data); summary(m2); confint(m2) m3 <- lm(gen_competent ~ belligerent * CWV_ctr + adapt_ctr, study4_data); summary(m3); confint(m3) m4 <- lm(org_competent ~ belligerent * CWV_ctr + adapt_ctr , study4_data); summary(m4); confint(m4) mismatch The code models general and organizational competence, not leadership effectiveness as described for the parallel regression in Study 4, even though proximal impact is included as a predictor alongside behavior, CWV, and their interaction. Thus the variable designated as the outcome in the code does not match the leadership effectiveness outcome specified in the paper for this analysis-dimension pairing. NA NA

In this case, it its hard to tell who was “right”. Due to inconsistent and partially incomprehensible naming of the variables the human rater “assumed” the variables to match even though the names were not unambiguous. CodeBot highlighted this as a mismatch which could also be valid. –> In cases like this, clearer criteria & a second rater could help. A sub-optimal option would be attempting to recalculate the script but that was a) explicitly not the focus of this work and b) creates a disproportional amount of extra work.

Standardize cumulative stressor cum_stress_11_z = as.numeric(scale(cum_stress_11, center = TRUE, scale = TRUE))) %>% # Convert variables to factors mutate(emp = as.factor(emp), sex = as.factor(sex), child = as.factor(child), group = as.factor(group), marpartner = as.factor(marpartner), race = as.factor(race), smoke_bio = as.factor(smoke_bio)) return(dataset) } # Apply the function to each dataset subset_dat1 <- transform_dataset(subset_dat1) subset_dat2 <- transform_dataset(subset_dat2) dat <- transform_dataset(dat) # PhenoAge main2 <-lm(eaa_pheno ~ # demographics age_bio_z + sex + edu_y_z + emp + race + marpartner + child + group + # health covariates smoke_bio + drinks_bio_z + bmi_bio_z + # key predictor cum_stress_11_z, data = subset_dat1) mismatch The code matches the described residualization of epigenetic age but does not clearly implement two stated transformations: standardizing all continuous variables (the outcome eaa_pheno is not z-scored) and constructing the cumulative stressor index by standardizing each of 11 domains before summing. Thus, the transformation steps in the code only partially align with those described in the paper. NA NA

Here, CodeBot seems to have spotted an issue the human coder missed. While the standardisation of cum_stress is in the code, the summing of the 11 variables which make up cumulative stress in the first place is not to be found. –> CodeBot might be especially helpful in cases where there is a big amount of Code in a format like this, which make it hard for humans to spot errors since it is so much code on so little lines.

helm 20 Binary logistic regression in Experiment 2 predicting assessor accuracy (correct vs. incorrect judgment of White witnesses’ identifications) solely from lineup race (same-race White vs. cross-race Black). The model showed a large effect (B=-1.69, SE=0.17, Wald=94.67, p<.001, OR=0.19), indicating that assessors were much less accurate when evaluating cross-race identifications by White witnesses than same-race identifications. Variable Specification To understand the relationships between the effects identified above and the ability of assessors to distinguish between accurate and inaccurate identification decisions, we conducted a binary logistic regression using lineup race to predict assessor accuracy. ... The model revealed a significant main effect of lineup race (B =− 1.69, SE = 0.17, Wald = 94.67, p , .001, OR = 0.19, 95% CI [0.13, 0.26]). As in Experiment 1, assessors were significantly less accurate in their assessments of cross-race identification decisions (M = 0.38, 95% CI [0.32, 0.43], SE = 0.14) than in their assessments of same-race identification decisions (M = 0.77, 95% CI [0.72, 0.81], SE = 0.24). # recode variables df$TargetRace = ifelse(df$TargetRace == 1, "White", "Black") ... df$Witness_accuracy = ifelse(df$Witness_correct == 1 & df$Accuracy == 1, 1, ifelse(df$Witness_correct == 0 & df$Accuracy == 0, 1, 0)) # Set reference levels df$Pick = as.factor(df$Pick) df$TargetRace = as.factor(df$TargetRace) df <- within(df, TargetRace <- relevel(TargetRace, ref = "White")) ... ### Binary logistic regression with target race only ### br1 = glm(Accuracy ~ TargetRace, data = df, family = "binomial") summary(br1) # odds ratios exp(cbind(coef(br1), confint(br1))) # main effect of lineup race br1_tr = wald.test(Sigma = vcov(br1), b = coef(br1), Terms = 2) round(br1_tr$result$chi2, 3) # Binary logistic regression with ID decision and target race br2 = glm(Accuracy ~ TargetRace*Pick, data = df, family = "binomial") summary(br2) # odds ratios exp(cbind(coef(br2), confint(br2))) # lineup identifications br2_id <- glm(Accuracy ~ TargetRace, data = df[df$Pick == "ID", ], family = "binomial") summary(br2_id) exp(cbind(coef(br2_id), confint(br2_id))) br2_idw = wald.test(Sigma = vcov(br2_id), b = coef(br2_id), Terms = 2) round(br2_idw$result$chi2, 5) mismatch The paper describes a logistic regression predicting assessor accuracy from lineup race, but the code uses the variable `Accuracy` as the outcome instead of the constructed `Witness_accuracy` variable that actually encodes assessor accuracy relative to ground truth, and it does not explicitly restrict to White witnesses as stated in the analysis description. NA NA

Based off re-inspection of the source code, I judge that CodeBot confused witness_accuracy with accuracy because it sounds more like what the authors are doing but does not truly reflect their actions since the variables aren’t always named unambiguously. –> Unclear variable naming was a reoccurring issue when screening the scripts.
